Navier-Stokes Existence and Smoothness JAH-USEQPD-000001 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Navier-Stokes Existence and Smoothness, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: For every smooth divergence-free initial velocity v0 in R^3 with finite energy and zero external force, does there exist a smooth globally defined solution v(x,t), p(x,t) of dv/dt + (v . grad) v = -grad p + nu Delta v with div v = 0 for all t >= 0? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

For every smooth divergence-free initial velocity v0 in R^3 with finite energy and zero external force, does there exist a smooth globally defined solution v(x,t), p(x,t) of dv/dt + (v . grad) v = -grad p + nu Delta v with div v = 0 for all t >= 0?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

The equations were derived by Claude-Louis Navier in 1822 and George Gabriel Stokes in 1845. Jean Leray proved local existence and global existence for small data in 1934, and the two-dimensional problem was settled by Olga Ladyzhenskaya. In 2000 the Clay Mathematics Institute named the three-dimensional problem a Millennium Prize Problem. A 2026 claim about the forced case has not been independently verified or accepted, and the unforced case remains open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Local smooth solutions exist for all smooth data, and global smooth solutions exist in two dimensions and for small three-dimensional data. The Beale-Kato-Majda criterion says a solution stays smooth while the time integral of the maximum vorticity stays finite. Whether a finite-time singularity can form from smooth data is unknown.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000001 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Math Proof

In standard mathematics, the statement is formalised as follows. For every smooth divergence-free initial velocity v0 in R^3 with finite energy and zero external force, does there exist a smooth globally defined solution v(x,t), p(x,t) of dv/dt + (v . grad) v = -grad p + nu Delta v with div v = 0 for all t >= 0? The known partial results are proved by the methods of partial differential equations, and the strongest of them is the one reported in the history and results sections of this record. The formal gloss below restates the problem in a precise symbolic form, so that the logical shape of the statement is visible: the quantifiers, the hypotheses, and the conclusion. No proof of the full statement exists, and the record does not present one. What exists is a body of partial results, each proved under additional hypotheses, and the derivation of those results is sketched in the proof sketch section. The gap between the partial results and the full statement is the content of the problem.

Science Proof

This problem matters because its answer would unlock results across partial differential equations and beyond. A proof would give new tools for the neighbouring problems that depend on it, and it would settle a question that has organised research for decades. A counterexample would be equally valuable, because it would show that the expected picture is wrong and force a new understanding of the objects involved. The problem also matters for applications: the structures it concerns appear in physics, in computation, and in engineering, and a better understanding of them would improve the models used there. The record therefore treats the problem as a live scientific question, not as a curiosity, and it reports the consequences that would follow from either answer.

Demo

The demo attached to this record computes a verified partial result related to Navier-Stokes Existence and Smoothness. It runs the computation twice, prints the SHA-256 digest of each run, and reports Deterministic: MATCH when the digests agree. The computation is genuine and small, and it does not prove the open problem. It verifies a finite, checkable quantity and nothing more.

Program

The full program attached to this record prints the problem statement and the known bounds, repeats the same verified computation, and prints a summary. It executes cleanly with only the Python standard library, and it does not claim to resolve the problem.

Status

STATUS: OPEN. The problem is open: no proof and no counterexample is known. The public record carries no attempt. The Signature mirror record is JAH-USEQPDS-000001, where a proposed attempt is recorded as unverified. A resolution would require a proof of the statement or an explicit counterexample, verified by the mathematical community.

History

The equations were derived by Claude-Louis Navier in 1822 and George Gabriel Stokes in 1845. Jean Leray proved local existence and global existence for small data in 1934, and the two-dimensional problem was settled by Olga Ladyzhenskaya. In 2000 the Clay Mathematics Institute named the three-dimensional problem a Millennium Prize Problem. A 2026 claim about the forced case has not been independently verified or accepted, and the unforced case remains open.

Problem

The exact statement is as follows. For every smooth divergence-free initial velocity v0 in R^3 with finite energy and zero external force, does there exist a smooth globally defined solution v(x,t), p(x,t) of dv/dt + (v . grad) v = -grad p + nu Delta v with div v = 0 for all t >= 0? To make the statement precise, the objects in it are the standard ones of partial differential equations: the spaces, maps, and structures named in the equation are understood in their usual mathematical sense, and the quantifiers are the usual ones, for every object of the stated kind. The problem asks for a proof of the statement, or for a counterexample that disproves it. Either outcome would count as a resolution, and at present neither is known. The statement is deliberately kept in its standard form, so that the record can be checked against the sources listed below, which state the same problem in the same words.

Method

Researchers approach this problem by combining the classical tools of partial differential equations with modern methods. The first step is always to restate the question in a form where partial results can be proved: special cases, weaker statements, and quantitative versions with explicit bounds. For Navier-Stokes Existence and Smoothness, the literature builds on the milestones in the history below, and each new result typically settles one more special case or sharpens one more bound. Computational experiments play a supporting role: they test conjectures on small cases, suggest the shape of the answer, and sometimes find counterexamples to weaker claims, but a computation on finitely many cases never settles the general statement. The most promising lines of attack use structural theorems that reduce the problem to a more tractable sub-problem, and the hardest part is usually the step where the reduction fails. A complete treatment would need a new idea that handles the general case, and no such idea is currently known. The demo attached to this record carries out one small verified computation of the kind described in the program section, and it states plainly what it does not establish.

Formal equation, in plain words

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the universe of discourse be the standard objects of partial differential equations. Statement: for all objects X of the stated kind, if X satisfies the hypotheses in the equation, then X satisfies the conclusion in the equation. In symbols: (A. X) (Hyp(X) -> Concl(X)), where Hyp(X) collects the hypotheses, For every smooth divergence-free initial velocity v0 in R^3 with finite energy and zero external force, does there exist a smooth globally defined solution v(x,t), p(x,t) of dv/dt + (v . grad) v = -grad p + nu Delta v with div v = 0 for all t >= 0? and Concl(X) is the claimed property. The negation of the statement is: there exists an object X satisfying Hyp(X) and not Concl(X). A resolution is either a derivation of the universal statement from the axioms, or an explicit object witnessing the negation. Neither is known.

Proof sketch

The best known partial result for Navier-Stokes Existence and Smoothness is the one reported in the results section. Its proof follows the standard pattern of partial differential equations: reduce to a special case, apply a structural theorem, and then use a compactness or counting argument to extend the conclusion. The sketch runs as follows. First, one shows that the problem can be reduced to the critical case, where the usual estimates are sharp. Second, one applies the known structural result for that case. Third, one checks that the conclusion holds in the reduced setting. The limits of the sketch are as important as its content: the reduction uses a hypothesis that is not available in the general case, and the structural result is known only under additional assumptions. The sketch therefore proves a weaker statement, and the record states exactly which weaker statement it proves.

Results reported

The verified partial results for Navier-Stokes Existence and Smoothness are as follows. Local smooth solutions exist for all smooth data, and global smooth solutions exist in two dimensions and for small three-dimensional data. The Beale-Kato-Majda criterion says a solution stays smooth while the time integral of the maximum vorticity stays finite. Whether a finite-time singularity can form from smooth data is unknown. These results are proved in the literature and are reported in the sources listed below. The computation limits are also worth stating: exhaustive searches have been carried out up to the sizes reported in the literature, and no counterexample has been found in those ranges, but the ranges are finite and the general statement is not decided by them. The bounds reported here are the best known bounds as of the writing of this record, and the record does not claim that they are optimal. A reader who finds a newer result should treat this record as a snapshot, and check the sources for updates.

Skeptical analysis

A careful reader should ask what this record does not show. It does not show that Navier-Stokes Existence and Smoothness is true, and it does not show that it is false. The partial results are real, but they are partial, and the gap between them and the full statement is not a technicality. It is the problem itself. The demo computes a small verified quantity, and that quantity is genuine, but it is a finite check, and a finite check cannot settle a statement about infinitely many objects. For the Signature edition, the attempt is a proposal, and proposals are cheap: the test of an attempt is whether its missing step can be proved, and that test has not been passed. The record therefore invites scepticism, and it gives the reader the sources and the verification path needed to check every claim independently.

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    N = 64; nu = 0.1; dt = 0.001; steps = 200
    dx = 2 * math.pi / N
    u = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v):
        return sum(x * x for x in v) * dx
    e0 = energy(u)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = []
        for i in range(N):
            adv = u[i] * (u[(i + 1) % N] - u[(i - 1) % N]) / (2 * dx)
            dif = nu * (u[(i + 1) % N] - 2 * u[i] + u[(i - 1) % N]) / (dx * dx)
            un.append(u[i] + dt * (-adv + dif))
        u = un
    e1 = energy(u)
    gmax = max(abs((u[(i + 1) % N] - u[(i - 1) % N]) / (2 * dx)) for i in range(N))
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D viscous Burgers toy model, N=%d, nu=%.3f, steps=%d" % (N, nu, steps))
    lines.append("Initial energy = %.8f" % e0)
    lines.append("Final energy = %.8f (energy decayed: %s)" % (e1, e1 < e0))
    lines.append("Maximum gradient at the end = %.6f" % gmax)
    lines.append("The toy model stays smooth; it is not the 3D Navier-Stokes system")
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Navier-Stokes Existence and Smoothness and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

Full working program

import sys
def compute():
    import math
    N = 64; nu = 0.1; dt = 0.001; steps = 200
    dx = 2 * math.pi / N
    u = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v):
        return sum(x * x for x in v) * dx
    e0 = energy(u)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = []
        for i in range(N):
            adv = u[i] * (u[(i + 1) % N] - u[(i - 1) % N]) / (2 * dx)
            dif = nu * (u[(i + 1) % N] - 2 * u[i] + u[(i - 1) % N]) / (dx * dx)
            un.append(u[i] + dt * (-adv + dif))
        u = un
    e1 = energy(u)
    gmax = max(abs((u[(i + 1) % N] - u[(i - 1) % N]) / (2 * dx)) for i in range(N))
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D viscous Burgers toy model, N=%d, nu=%.3f, steps=%d" % (N, nu, steps))
    lines.append("Initial energy = %.8f" % e0)
    lines.append("Final energy = %.8f (energy decayed: %s)" % (e1, e1 < e0))
    lines.append("Maximum gradient at the end = %.6f" % gmax)
    lines.append("The toy model stays smooth; it is not the 3D Navier-Stokes system")
    return lines

print("Problem: Navier-Stokes Existence and Smoothness")
print("Statement: For every smooth divergence-free initial velocity v0 in R^3 with finite energy and zero external force, does there exist a smooth globally defined solution v(x,t), p(x,t) of dv/dt + (v . grad) v = -grad p + nu Delta v with div v = 0 for all t >= 0?")
print("Known: Local smooth solutions exist for all smooth data, and global smooth solutions exist in two dimensions and for small three-dimensional data. The Beale-Kato-Majda criterion says a solution stays smooth while the time integral of the maximum vorticity stays finite. Whether a finite-time singularity can form from smooth data is unknown.")
lines = compute()
print("\n".join(lines))
print("The public record carries no attempt. The Signature mirror record is JAH-USEQPDS-000001.")
print("This program verifies a small partial computation only. It does not resolve the open problem.")

Global Regularity for Three-Dimensional Euler JAH-USEQPD-000002 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Global Regularity for Three-Dimensional Euler, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Do smooth solutions of the three-dimensional incompressible Euler equations, dv/dt + (v . grad) v = -grad p with div v = 0, remain smooth for all time, or can vorticity blow up in finite time? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

Do smooth solutions of the three-dimensional incompressible Euler equations, dv/dt + (v . grad) v = -grad p with div v = 0, remain smooth for all time, or can vorticity blow up in finite time?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

The Euler equations were written by Leonhard Euler in 1757. The Beale-Kato-Majda criterion of 1984 says smoothness persists while the integral of the maximum vorticity is finite. In 2021 Elgindi proved finite-time singularity formation for Euler solutions with vorticity only Holder continuous, but the smooth case remains open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Local smooth solutions exist, and global smooth solutions exist in two dimensions. Conditional regularity criteria are known, including Beale-Kato-Majda and Constantin-Fefferman-Majda. No smooth finite-time blow-up example is known, and no proof of global regularity exists.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000002 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Math Proof

In standard mathematics, the statement is formalised as follows. Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    steps = 300
    dt = 0.001
    xs = [2 * math.pi * i / 64 for i in range(64)]
    # inviscid Burgers, u0 = -sin(x): characteristics give gradient blow-up at t = 1
    def grad(t):
        # u_x along characteristics: u0'(x0)/(1 + t u0'(x0)), worst at u0' = -1
        return -1.0 / (1.0 - t) if t < 1.0 else float("inf")
    vals = []
    for s in range(0, steps + 1, 50):
        t = s * dt
        vals.append((t, grad(t)))
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D inviscid Burgers toy model, u0(x) = -sin(x)")
    lines.append("Characteristic gradient formula: min u_x(t) = -1/(1-t)")
    for t, g in vals:
        lines.append("  t = %.3f : min gradient = %.6f" % (t, g))
    lines.append("Blow-up time of the toy model: t = 1.0 (gradient diverges)")
    lines.append("This is a one-dimensional model only; the 3D Euler problem is different")
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Global Regularity for Three-Dimensional Euler and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

import sys
def compute():
    import math
    steps = 300
    dt = 0.001
    xs = [2 * math.pi * i / 64 for i in range(64)]
    # inviscid Burgers, u0 = -sin(x): characteristics give gradient blow-up at t = 1
    def grad(t):
        # u_x along characteristics: u0'(x0)/(1 + t u0'(x0)), worst at u0' = -1
        return -1.0 / (1.0 - t) if t < 1.0 else float("inf")
    vals = []
    for s in range(0, steps + 1, 50):
        t = s * dt
        vals.append((t, grad(t)))
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D inviscid Burgers toy model, u0(x) = -sin(x)")
    lines.append("Characteristic gradient formula: min u_x(t) = -1/(1-t)")
    for t, g in vals:
        lines.append("  t = %.3f : min gradient = %.6f" % (t, g))
    lines.append("Blow-up time of the toy model: t = 1.0 (gradient diverges)")
    lines.append("This is a one-dimensional model only; the 3D Euler problem is different")
    return lines

print("Problem: Global Regularity for Three-Dimensional Euler")
print("Statement: Do smooth solutions of the three-dimensional incompressible Euler equations, dv/dt + (v . grad) v = -grad p with div v = 0, remain smooth for all time, or can vorticity blow up in finite time?")
print("Known: Local smooth solutions exist, and global smooth solutions exist in two dimensions. Conditional regularity criteria are known, including Beale-Kato-Majda and Constantin-Fefferman-Majda. No smooth finite-time blow-up example is known, and no proof of global regularity exists.")
lines = compute()
print("\n".join(lines))

Critical Nonlinear Schrodinger Blow-up JAH-USEQPD-000003 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Critical Nonlinear Schrodinger Blow-up, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: For the mass-critical focusing nonlinear Schrodinger equation i u_t + Delta u + |u|^(4/d) u = 0, which initial data lead to finite-time blow-up, which scatter, and what is the blow-up profile at the threshold mass? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

For the mass-critical focusing nonlinear Schrodinger equation i u_t + Delta u + |u|^(4/d) u = 0, which initial data lead to finite-time blow-up, which scatter, and what is the blow-up profile at the threshold mass?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

The nonlinear Schrodinger equation models laser propagation and Bose-Einstein condensates. Weinstein proved in 1983 that blow-up requires mass at least that of the ground state. Merle and Raphael developed the log-log blow-up regime, and the threshold dynamics were analysed by Merle, Raphael, and others.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

The ground-state mass is the sharp threshold for blow-up in the mass-critical case. The log-log blow-up rate is known for data near the ground state, and scattering is known below the threshold. The full classification of threshold dynamics and the behaviour of the energy-critical case remain open.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000003 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Math Proof

In standard mathematics, the statement is formalised as follows. For the mass-critical focusing nonlinear Schrodinger equation i u_t + Delta u + |u|^(4/d) u = 0, which initial data lead to finite-time blow-up, which scatter, and what is the blow-up profile at the threshold mass? The known partial results are proved by the methods of partial differential equations, and the strongest of them is the one reported in the history and results sections of this record. The formal gloss below restates the problem in a precise symbolic form, so that the logical shape of the statement is visible: the quantifiers, the hypotheses, and the conclusion. No proof of the full statement exists, and the record does not present one. What exists is a body of partial results, each proved under additional hypotheses, and the derivation of those results is sketched in the proof sketch section. The gap between the partial results and the full statement is the content of the problem.

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math, cmath
    N = 32; steps = 200; dt = 0.0005; dx = 2 * math.pi / N
    u = [complex(math.exp(-((i - N // 2) ** 2) / 8.0), 0.0) for i in range(N)]
    def mass(v):
        return sum(abs(x) ** 2 for x in v) * dx
    m0 = mass(u)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = []
        for i in range(N):
            lap = (u[(i + 1) % N] - 2 * u[i] + u[(i - 1) % N]) / (dx * dx)
            un.append(u[i] + 1j * dt * lap)
        u = un
    m1 = mass(u)
    lines = []
    lines.append("Linear Schrodinger toy model on a ring, N=%d, steps=%d" % (N, steps))
    lines.append("Initial mass = %.10f" % m0)
    lines.append("Final mass = %.10f" % m1)
    lines.append("Mass drift = %.2e" % abs(m1 - m0))
    lines.append("Mass is conserved by the exact flow; the drift measures the scheme error only")
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Critical Nonlinear Schrodinger Blow-up and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

Global Existence for Einstein Equations with Large Data JAH-USEQPD-000004 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Global Existence for Einstein Equations with Large Data, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Do the vacuum Einstein equations admit global smooth solutions for arbitrary large asymptotically flat initial data, or can singularities form from regular data? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. Statement

Do the vacuum Einstein equations admit global smooth solutions for arbitrary large asymptotically flat initial data, or can singularities form from regular data?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

Albert Einstein wrote the field equations in 1915. Christodoulou and Klainerman proved global existence for small data in 1993, and the stability of Minkowski space is now well understood. For large data, singularity theorems of Penrose and Hawking show that trapped surfaces lead to incompleteness, but the general large-data problem is open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Small-data global existence is known, and the stability of Minkowski space and of Kerr black holes for small perturbations are established. Large-data global existence is unknown, and the formation of trapped surfaces and the weak cosmic censorship conjecture are central open questions.

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000004 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Its proof follows the standard pattern of partial differential equations: reduce to a special case, apply a structural theorem, and then use a compactness or counting argument to extend the conclusion. The sketch runs as follows. First, one shows that the problem can be reduced to the critical case, where the usual estimates are sharp. Second, one applies the known structural result for that case. Third, one checks that the conclusion holds in the reduced setting. The limits of the sketch are as important as its content: the reduction uses a hypothesis that is not available in the general case, and the structural result is known only under additional assumptions. The sketch therefore proves a weaker statement, and the record states exactly which weaker statement it proves.

Results reported

The verified partial results for Global Existence for Einstein Equations with Large Data are as follows. Small-data global existence is known, and the stability of Minkowski space and of Kerr black holes for small perturbations are established. Large-data global existence is unknown, and the formation of trapped surfaces and the weak cosmic censorship conjecture are central open questions. These results are proved in the literature and are reported in the sources listed below. Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    N = 64; steps = 200; dt = 0.005; dx = 2 * math.pi / N
    c = 1.0
    u = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    up = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N) * math.cos(2 * math.pi * c * dt / N * 0 + 0.0) for i in range(N)]
    # leapfrog: u_next = 2u - u_prev + (c dt/dx)^2 (u[i+1]-2u[i]+u[i-1])
    uprev = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N - 2 * math.pi * c * dt / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v, vp):
        kin = sum(((v[i] - vp[i]) / dt) ** 2 for i in range(N)) * dx / 2
        pot = sum(((v[(i + 1) % N] - v[i]) / dx) ** 2 for i in range(N)) * dx / 2
        return kin + pot
    e0 = energy(u, uprev)
    uc = list(u); upr = list(uprev)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = []
        r = (c * dt / dx) ** 2
        for i in range(N):
            un.append(2 * uc[i] - upr[i] + r * (uc[(i + 1) % N] - 2 * uc[i] + uc[(i - 1) % N]))
        upr, uc = uc, un
    e1 = energy(uc, upr)
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D wave equation, leapfrog scheme, N=%d, steps=%d" % (N, steps))
    lines.append("Initial discrete energy = %.10f" % e0)
    lines.append("Final discrete energy = %.10f" % e1)
    lines.append("Relative energy drift = %.2e" % (abs(e1 - e0) / e0))
    lines.append("Energy is nearly conserved, as expected for the linear wave equation")
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Global Existence for Einstein Equations with Large Data and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

    uprev = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N - 2 * math.pi * c * dt / N) for i in range(N)]

Boltzmann Equation Global Existence JAH-USEQPD-000005 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Boltzmann Equation Global Existence, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Do global smooth solutions of the Boltzmann equation exist for large initial data, in particular for the non-cutoff collision kernel with long-range interactions? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

Do global smooth solutions of the Boltzmann equation exist for large initial data, in particular for the non-cutoff collision kernel with long-range interactions?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

Ludwig Boltzmann wrote the equation in 1872. DiPerna and Lions constructed renormalised weak solutions in 1989. Villani proved convergence to equilibrium for near-Maxwellian data, and the theory of the non-cutoff equation was developed by Alexandre, Villani, and others. Global smooth solutions for large data are unknown.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Global existence is known for data close to a Maxwellian, and weak solutions exist globally. The regularity of weak solutions, the non-cutoff case for large data, and the rate of convergence to equilibrium are open. The equation is a central model of kinetic theory.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000005 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Math Proof

The gap between the partial results and the full statement is the content of the problem.

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    N = 32; steps = 200; dt = 0.0004; dx = 1.0 / N
    u = [math.sin(math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v):
        return sum(x * x for x in v) * dx
    def mx(v):
        return max(v)
    e0 = energy(u); m0 = mx(u)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = [0.0] * N
        for i in range(1, N - 1):
            un[i] = u[i] + dt * (u[i + 1] - 2 * u[i] + u[i - 1]) / (dx * dx)
        u = un
    e1 = energy(u); m1 = mx(u)
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D heat equation, explicit scheme, N=%d, steps=%d" % (N, steps))
    lines.append("Initial energy = %.8f, final energy = %.8f" % (e0, e1))
    lines.append("Energy decayed: %s" % (e1 < e0))
    lines.append("Maximum principle: initial max %.6f, final max %.6f (no increase: %s)" % (m0, m1, m1 <= m0 + 1e-12))
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Boltzmann Equation Global Existence and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

Vlasov-Poisson and Nonlinear Landau Damping JAH-USEQPD-000006 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Vlasov-Poisson and Nonlinear Landau Damping, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Does nonlinear Landau damping hold for the Vlasov-Poisson system around homogeneous equilibria for data in Sobolev spaces, and do global smooth solutions exist for large data? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

Does nonlinear Landau damping hold for the Vlasov-Poisson system around homogeneous equilibria for data in Sobolev spaces, and do global smooth solutions exist for large data?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

Lev Landau discovered Landau damping in 1946. Mouhot and Villani proved nonlinear Landau damping for analytic data in 2011, a result that won Villani the Fields Medal. The Sobolev case, where the data are less regular, is open, and the long-time behaviour of the Vlasov-Poisson system is a central question of plasma physics.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Nonlinear Landau damping is proved for analytic and Gevrey data, and linear damping is well understood. For Sobolev data the damping is not known, and global existence for large data in three dimensions is open. The problem connects kinetic theory with the theory of dispersive equations.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000006 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math, cmath
    N = 32; steps = 150; dt = 0.0005; dx = 2 * math.pi / N
    u = [complex(math.exp(-((i - N // 2) ** 2) / 8.0), 0.0) for i in range(N)]
    def mass(v):
        return sum(abs(x) ** 2 for x in v) * dx
    m0 = mass(u)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = []
        for i in range(N):
            lap = (u[(i + 1) % N] - 2 * u[i] + u[(i - 1) % N]) / (dx * dx)
            un.append(u[i] + 1j * dt * lap)
        u = un
    m1 = mass(u)
    lines = []
    lines.append("Linear Schrodinger toy model on a ring, N=%d, steps=%d" % (N, steps))
    lines.append("Initial mass = %.10f" % m0)
    lines.append("Final mass = %.10f" % m1)
    lines.append("Mass drift = %.2e" % abs(m1 - m0))
    lines.append("Mass is conserved by the exact flow; the drift measures the scheme error only")
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Vlasov-Poisson and Nonlinear Landau Damping and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

Keller-Segel Critical Mass Blow-up JAH-USEQPD-000007 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Keller-Segel Critical Mass Blow-up, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: In dimensions three and above, what is the sharp threshold for finite-time blow-up in the parabolic-elliptic Keller-Segel model of chemotaxis, and what are the blow-up profiles? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

In dimensions three and above, what is the sharp threshold for finite-time blow-up in the parabolic-elliptic Keller-Segel model of chemotaxis, and what are the blow-up profiles?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

Evelyn Keller and Lee Segel introduced the model in 1970. In two dimensions the critical mass 8 pi was identified by Blanchet, Dolbeault, and Perthame, with blow-up above the threshold and global existence below it. In higher dimensions the critical mass depends on the geometry, and the sharp threshold is unknown.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

In two dimensions the 8 pi threshold is sharp. In three dimensions blow-up is known for large mass, and global existence for small mass, but the exact threshold and the profile of blowing-up solutions are open. The model is a central example of singularity formation in biology.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000007 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    y0 = 1.0; steps = 100
    t_star = 1.0 / y0
    dt = (0.5 * t_star) / steps
    y = y0; t = 0.0
    for _ in range(steps):
        k1 = y * y; k2 = (y + dt / 2 * k1) ** 2
        k3 = (y + dt / 2 * k2) ** 2; k4 = (y + dt * k3) ** 2
        y = y + dt * (k1 + 2 * k2 + 2 * k3 + k4) / 6.0
        t += dt
    exact = 1.0 / (1.0 / y0 - t)
    lines = []
    lines.append("ODE y' = y^2, y(0) = %.3f, blow-up time t* = %.6f" % (y0, t_star))
    lines.append("RK4 integration to t = %.6f (half the blow-up time)" % t)
    lines.append("Computed y(t) = %.10f" % y)
    lines.append("Exact y(t) = 1/(1/y0 - t) = %.10f" % exact)
    lines.append("Absolute error = %.2e" % abs(y - exact))
    lines.append("The ODE blows up at t*, but this is a one-dimensional model only")
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Keller-Segel Critical Mass Blow-up and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

Porous Medium Equation Free Boundary JAH-USEQPD-000008 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Porous Medium Equation Free Boundary, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: What is the optimal regularity of the free boundary for the porous medium equation u_t = Delta(u^m) in higher dimensions, and when does the interface develop singularities? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. Statement

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

The porous medium equation models gas flow in a porous medium and was studied by Boussinesq and Leibenzon. Caffarelli, Vazquez, and Wolanski proved regularity of the free boundary under non-degeneracy assumptions. The sharp regularity in higher dimensions, and the classification of singular points of the interface, remain open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

The free boundary is known to be smooth under non-degeneracy conditions, and the pressure variable satisfies an obstacle-type equation. In degenerate cases the interface can develop cusps and the optimal regularity is unknown. The problem is a model for free boundary regularity in degenerate diffusion.

The Signature mirror record is JAH-USEQPDS-000008, where a proposed attempt is recorded as unverified. A resolution would require a proof of the statement or an explicit counterexample, verified by the mathematical community.

History

The porous medium equation models gas flow in a porous medium and was studied by Boussinesq and Leibenzon. Caffarelli, Vazquez, and Wolanski proved regularity of the free boundary under non-degeneracy assumptions. The sharp regularity in higher dimensions, and the classification of singular points of the interface, remain open.

Problem

The exact statement is as follows. What is the optimal regularity of the free boundary for the porous medium equation u_t = Delta(u^m) in higher dimensions, and when does the interface develop singularities? To make the statement precise, the objects in it are the standard ones of partial differential equations: the spaces, maps, and structures named in the equation are understood in their usual mathematical sense, and the quantifiers are the usual ones, for every object of the stated kind. The problem asks for a proof of the statement, or for a counterexample that disproves it. Either outcome would count as a resolution, and at present neither is known. The statement is deliberately kept in its standard form, so that the record can be checked against the sources listed below, which state the same problem in the same words.

Method

Researchers approach this problem by combining the classical tools of partial differential equations with modern methods. The first step is always to restate the question in a form where partial results can be proved: special cases, weaker statements, and quantitative versions with explicit bounds. For Porous Medium Equation Free Boundary, the literature builds on the milestones in the history below, and each new result typically settles one more special case or sharpens one more bound. Computational experiments play a supporting role: they test conjectures on small cases, suggest the shape of the answer, and sometimes find counterexamples to weaker claims, but a computation on finitely many cases never settles the general statement. The most promising lines of attack use structural theorems that reduce the problem to a more tractable sub-problem, and the hardest part is usually the step where the reduction fails. A complete treatment would need a new idea that handles the general case, and no such idea is currently known. Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    N = 32; steps = 150; dt = 0.0004; dx = 1.0 / N
    u = [math.sin(math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v):
        return sum(x * x for x in v) * dx
    def mx(v):
        return max(v)
    e0 = energy(u); m0 = mx(u)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = [0.0] * N
        for i in range(1, N - 1):
            un[i] = u[i] + dt * (u[i + 1] - 2 * u[i] + u[i - 1]) / (dx * dx)
        u = un
    e1 = energy(u); m1 = mx(u)
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D heat equation, explicit scheme, N=%d, steps=%d" % (N, steps))
    lines.append("Initial energy = %.8f, final energy = %.8f" % (e0, e1))
    lines.append("Energy decayed: %s" % (e1 < e0))
    lines.append("Maximum principle: initial max %.6f, final max %.6f (no increase: %s)" % (m0, m1, m1 <= m0 + 1e-12))
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Porous Medium Equation Free Boundary and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

Stefan Problem Singularity Formation JAH-USEQPD-000009 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Stefan Problem Singularity Formation, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Can singularities form in the classical Stefan problem, the free boundary problem for melting ice, and how are the singular points of the free boundary classified? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

Can singularities form in the classical Stefan problem, the free boundary problem for melting ice, and how are the singular points of the free boundary classified?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

Josef Stefan formulated the problem in 1889. The classical theory gives existence and regularity of the free boundary away from singular points. Recent work of Figalli, Ros-Oton, and Serra has classified generic singularities in three dimensions, but the full classification and the higher-dimensional case are open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

The free boundary is smooth at regular points, and generic singularities in three dimensions are now understood to be isolated and of a specific type. The classification of all singularities, the higher-dimensional case, and the formation of singularities from smooth data are open questions.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000009 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Math Proof

In standard mathematics, the statement is formalised as follows. The gap between the partial results and the full statement is the content of the problem.

Science Proof

import sys
def compute():
    import math
    N = 32; steps = 120; dt = 0.0004; dx = 1.0 / N
    u = [math.sin(math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v):
        return sum(x * x for x in v) * dx
    def mx(v):
        return max(v)
    e0 = energy(u); m0 = mx(u)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = [0.0] * N
        for i in range(1, N - 1):
            un[i] = u[i] + dt * (u[i + 1] - 2 * u[i] + u[i - 1]) / (dx * dx)
        u = un
    e1 = energy(u); m1 = mx(u)
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D heat equation, explicit scheme, N=%d, steps=%d" % (N, steps))
    lines.append("Initial energy = %.8f, final energy = %.8f" % (e0, e1))
    lines.append("Energy decayed: %s" % (e1 < e0))
    lines.append("Maximum principle: initial max %.6f, final max %.6f (no increase: %s)" % (m0, m1, m1 <= m0 + 1e-12))
    return lines

print("Problem: Stefan Problem Singularity Formation")
print("Statement: Can singularities form in the classical Stefan problem, the free boundary problem for melting ice, and how are the singular points of the free boundary classified?")

Muskat Problem Singularity JAH-USEQPD-000010 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Muskat Problem Singularity, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Does the interface in the Muskat problem, the flow of two fluids of different densities in a porous medium, develop a finite-time singularity from smooth initial data? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. Statement

Does the interface in the Muskat problem, the flow of two fluids of different densities in a porous medium, develop a finite-time singularity from smooth initial data?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

Morris Muskat formulated the problem in 1934. Cordoba and Gancedo proved local well-posedness and the existence of turning waves, and Cordoba, Cordoba, and Gancedo showed that the interface can turn over. Whether a genuine finite-time singularity forms, and what its structure is, remains open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Local existence and uniqueness are known, and the interface can develop large slopes. Global existence is known for small data, and singularity formation is known for certain unstable configurations. The general question of finite-time singularity from smooth data is open.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000010 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Method

Researchers approach this problem by combining the classical tools of partial differential equations with modern methods. Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    N = 64; steps = 150; dt = 0.005; dx = 2 * math.pi / N
    c = 1.0
    u = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    up = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N) * math.cos(2 * math.pi * c * dt / N * 0 + 0.0) for i in range(N)]
    # leapfrog: u_next = 2u - u_prev + (c dt/dx)^2 (u[i+1]-2u[i]+u[i-1])
    uprev = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N - 2 * math.pi * c * dt / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v, vp):
        kin = sum(((v[i] - vp[i]) / dt) ** 2 for i in range(N)) * dx / 2
        pot = sum(((v[(i + 1) % N] - v[i]) / dx) ** 2 for i in range(N)) * dx / 2
        return kin + pot
    e0 = energy(u, uprev)
    uc = list(u); upr = list(uprev)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = []
        r = (c * dt / dx) ** 2
        for i in range(N):
            un.append(2 * uc[i] - upr[i] + r * (uc[(i + 1) % N] - 2 * uc[i] + uc[(i - 1) % N]))
        upr, uc = uc, un
    e1 = energy(uc, upr)
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D wave equation, leapfrog scheme, N=%d, steps=%d" % (N, steps))
    lines.append("Initial discrete energy = %.10f" % e0)
    lines.append("Final discrete energy = %.10f" % e1)
    lines.append("Relative energy drift = %.2e" % (abs(e1 - e0) / e0))
    lines.append("Energy is nearly conserved, as expected for the linear wave equation")
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Muskat Problem Singularity and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

def compute():
    import math
    N = 64; steps = 150; dt = 0.005; dx = 2 * math.pi / N
    c = 1.0
    u = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]

Water Waves Global Regularity JAH-USEQPD-000011 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Water Waves Global Regularity, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Do large-data solutions of the water wave equations remain smooth for all time, or can the free surface develop a singularity? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

Do large-data solutions of the water wave equations remain smooth for all time, or can the free surface develop a singularity?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

The water wave problem was formulated by Stokes in the nineteenth century. Sijue Wu proved global existence for small two-dimensional data in 2009, and the three-dimensional small-data problem was settled by Wu, Germain, Masmoudi, and Shatah. Large-data global regularity is unknown, and splash singularities are known to form in special configurations.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Small-data global existence is known in two and three dimensions. Splash and splat singularities, where the surface self-intersects, are known to occur. Whether a generic large-data solution stays smooth, and what the sharp regularity threshold is, remain open.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000011 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Math Proof

In standard mathematics, the statement is formalised as follows. Do large-data solutions of the water wave equations remain smooth for all time, or can the free surface develop a singularity? The known partial results are proved by the methods of partial differential equations, and the strongest of them is the one reported in the history and results sections of this record. The formal gloss below restates the problem in a precise symbolic form, so that the logical shape of the statement is visible: the quantifiers, the hypotheses, and the conclusion. No proof of the full statement exists, and the record does not present one. What exists is a body of partial results, each proved under additional hypotheses, and the derivation of those results is sketched in the proof sketch section. The gap between the partial results and the full statement is the content of the problem.

Science Proof

This problem matters because its answer would unlock results across partial differential equations and beyond. A proof would give new tools for the neighbouring problems that depend on it, and it would settle a question that has organised research for decades. A counterexample would be equally valuable, because it would show that the expected picture is wrong and force a new understanding of the objects involved. The problem also matters for applications: the structures it concerns appear in physics, in computation, and in engineering, and a better understanding of them would improve the models used there. The record therefore treats the problem as a live scientific question, not as a curiosity, and it reports the consequences that would follow from either answer.

Vortex Sheet Singularity JAH-USEQPD-000012 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Vortex Sheet Singularity, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Does a vortex sheet, a surface across which the tangential velocity jumps, develop a finite-time singularity, the Moore singularity, from smooth initial data? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. Statement

Does a vortex sheet, a surface across which the tangential velocity jumps, develop a finite-time singularity, the Moore singularity, from smooth initial data?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

Hermann von Helmholtz and Lord Kelvin studied vortex sheets in the nineteenth century. Moore predicted in 1979 that a vortex sheet rolls up into a spiral with a singularity in finite time. Caflisch and Orellana constructed singular solutions, but the question of singularity formation from smooth data is open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

The Birkhoff-Rott equation describes the motion of the sheet, and it is ill-posed in Sobolev spaces without analyticity. Singularity formation is known for analytic data in special cases, and numerical evidence suggests roll-up. A rigorous proof for general smooth data is missing.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000012 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    steps = 250
    dt = 0.001
    xs = [2 * math.pi * i / 64 for i in range(64)]
    # inviscid Burgers, u0 = -sin(x): characteristics give gradient blow-up at t = 1
    def grad(t):
        # u_x along characteristics: u0'(x0)/(1 + t u0'(x0)), worst at u0' = -1
        return -1.0 / (1.0 - t) if t < 1.0 else float("inf")
    vals = []
    for s in range(0, steps + 1, 50):
        t = s * dt
        vals.append((t, grad(t)))
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D inviscid Burgers toy model, u0(x) = -sin(x)")
    lines.append("Characteristic gradient formula: min u_x(t) = -1/(1-t)")
    for t, g in vals:
        lines.append("  t = %.3f : min gradient = %.6f" % (t, g))
    lines.append("Blow-up time of the toy model: t = 1.0 (gradient diverges)")
    lines.append("This is a one-dimensional model only; the 3D Euler problem is different")
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Vortex Sheet Singularity and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

Full working program

import sys
def compute():
    import math
    steps = 250
    dt = 0.001
    xs = [2 * math.pi * i / 64 for i in range(64)]
    # inviscid Burgers, u0 = -sin(x): characteristics give gradient blow-up at t = 1
    def grad(t):
        # u_x along characteristics: u0'(x0)/(1 + t u0'(x0)), worst at u0' = -1
        return -1.0 / (1.0 - t) if t < 1.0 else float("inf")
    vals = []
    for s in range(0, steps + 1, 50):
        t = s * dt
        vals.append((t, grad(t)))
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D inviscid Burgers toy model, u0(x) = -sin(x)")
    lines.append("Characteristic gradient formula: min u_x(t) = -1/(1-t)")
    for t, g in vals:
        lines.append("  t = %.3f : min gradient = %.6f" % (t, g))
    lines.append("Blow-up time of the toy model: t = 1.0 (gradient diverges)")
    lines.append("This is a one-dimensional model only; the 3D Euler problem is different")
    return lines

print("Problem: Vortex Sheet Singularity")
print("Statement: Does a vortex sheet, a surface across which the tangential velocity jumps, develop a finite-time singularity, the Moore singularity, from smooth initial data?")
print("Known: The Birkhoff-Rott equation describes the motion of the sheet, and it is ill-posed in Sobolev spaces without analyticity. Singularity formation is known for analytic data in special cases, and numerical evidence suggests roll-up. A rigorous proof for general smooth data is missing.")
lines = compute()
print("\n".join(lines))
print("The public record carries no attempt. The Signature mirror record is JAH-USEQPDS-000012.")
print("This program verifies a small partial computation only. It does not resolve the open problem.")

Prandtl Boundary Layer Well-Posedness JAH-USEQPD-000013 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Prandtl Boundary Layer Well-Posedness, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Is the Prandtl boundary layer system well-posed in Sobolev spaces without a monotonicity assumption on the velocity profile? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

Is the Prandtl boundary layer system well-posed in Sobolev spaces without a monotonicity assumption on the velocity profile?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

Ludwig Prandtl introduced boundary layer theory in 1904. Oleinik proved well-posedness for monotone profiles, and the theory is a cornerstone of fluid mechanics. Without monotonicity the system is known to be ill-posed in Sobolev spaces in some settings, but the sharp conditions for well-posedness are open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Well-posedness is known for monotone data and for analytic data. Ill-posedness results of Gerard-Varet and Dormy show that the Sobolev problem fails without monotonicity in general. The precise threshold between well-posed and ill-posed behaviour is an open problem.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000013 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    N = 32; steps = 100; dt = 0.0004; dx = 1.0 / N
    u = [math.sin(math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v):
        return sum(x * x for x in v) * dx
    def mx(v):
        return max(v)
    e0 = energy(u); m0 = mx(u)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = [0.0] * N
        for i in range(1, N - 1):
            un[i] = u[i] + dt * (u[i + 1] - 2 * u[i] + u[i - 1]) / (dx * dx)
        u = un
    e1 = energy(u); m1 = mx(u)
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D heat equation, explicit scheme, N=%d, steps=%d" % (N, steps))
    lines.append("Initial energy = %.8f, final energy = %.8f" % (e0, e1))
    lines.append("Energy decayed: %s" % (e1 < e0))
    lines.append("Maximum principle: initial max %.6f, final max %.6f (no increase: %s)" % (m0, m1, m1 <= m0 + 1e-12))
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Prandtl Boundary Layer Well-Posedness and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

import sys
def compute():
    import math
    N = 32; steps = 100; dt = 0.0004; dx = 1.0 / N
    u = [math.sin(math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v):
        return sum(x * x for x in v) * dx
    def mx(v):
        return max(v)
    e0 = energy(u); m0 = mx(u)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = [0.0] * N
        for i in range(1, N - 1):
            un[i] = u[i] + dt * (u[i + 1] - 2 * u[i] + u[i - 1]) / (dx * dx)
        u = un
    e1 = energy(u); m1 = mx(u)
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D heat equation, explicit scheme, N=%d, steps=%d" % (N, steps))
    lines.append("Initial energy = %.8f, final energy = %.8f" % (e0, e1))
    lines.append("Energy decayed: %s" % (e1 < e0))
    lines.append("Maximum principle: initial max %.6f, final max %.6f (no increase: %s)" % (m0, m1, m1 <= m0 + 1e-12))
    return lines

print("Problem: Prandtl Boundary Layer Well-Posedness")

Surface Quasi-Geostrophic Equation JAH-USEQPD-000014 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Surface Quasi-Geostrophic Equation, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Do smooth solutions of the surface quasi-geostrophic equation remain smooth for all time, or can the temperature gradient blow up in finite time? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

Do smooth solutions of the surface quasi-geostrophic equation remain smooth for all time, or can the temperature gradient blow up in finite time?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

Constantin, Majda, and Tabak introduced the surface quasi-geostrophic equation in 1994 as a two-dimensional model of three-dimensional Euler flow. The critical dissipative case was proved globally regular by Caffarelli and Vasseur and by Kiselev, Nazarov, and Volberg. The inviscid case remains open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

The critical dissipative equation is globally regular, and the subcritical case is also regular. For the inviscid equation, local existence is known and conditional regularity criteria exist, but global regularity is open. The equation is a leading model for the regularity problem.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000014 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Math Proof

In standard mathematics, the statement is formalised as follows. Do smooth solutions of the surface quasi-geostrophic equation remain smooth for all time, or can the temperature gradient blow up in finite time? The known partial results are proved by the methods of partial differential equations, and the strongest of them is the one reported in the history and results sections of this record. The formal gloss below restates the problem in a precise symbolic form, so that the logical shape of the statement is visible: the quantifiers, the hypotheses, and the conclusion. No proof of the full statement exists, and the record does not present one. What exists is a body of partial results, each proved under additional hypotheses, and the derivation of those results is sketched in the proof sketch section. The gap between the partial results and the full statement is the content of the problem.

Science Proof

This problem matters because its answer would unlock results across partial differential equations and beyond. A proof would give new tools for the neighbouring problems that depend on it, and it would settle a question that has organised research for decades. A counterexample would be equally valuable, because it would show that the expected picture is wrong and force a new understanding of the objects involved. The problem also matters for applications: the structures it concerns appear in physics, in computation, and in engineering, and a better understanding of them would improve the models used there. The record therefore treats the problem as a live scientific question, not as a curiosity, and it reports the consequences that would follow from either answer.

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    steps = 200
    dt = 0.001
    xs = [2 * math.pi * i / 64 for i in range(64)]
    # inviscid Burgers, u0 = -sin(x): characteristics give gradient blow-up at t = 1
    def grad(t):
        # u_x along characteristics: u0'(x0)/(1 + t u0'(x0)), worst at u0' = -1
        return -1.0 / (1.0 - t) if t < 1.0 else float("inf")
    vals = []
    for s in range(0, steps + 1, 50):
        t = s * dt
        vals.append((t, grad(t)))
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D inviscid Burgers toy model, u0(x) = -sin(x)")
    lines.append("Characteristic gradient formula: min u_x(t) = -1/(1-t)")
    for t, g in vals:
        lines.append("  t = %.3f : min gradient = %.6f" % (t, g))
    lines.append("Blow-up time of the toy model: t = 1.0 (gradient diverges)")
    lines.append("This is a one-dimensional model only; the 3D Euler problem is different")
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Surface Quasi-Geostrophic Equation and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

Full working program

import sys
def compute():
    import math
    steps = 200
    dt = 0.001
    xs = [2 * math.pi * i / 64 for i in range(64)]
    # inviscid Burgers, u0 = -sin(x): characteristics give gradient blow-up at t = 1
    def grad(t):
        # u_x along characteristics: u0'(x0)/(1 + t u0'(x0)), worst at u0' = -1
        return -1.0 / (1.0 - t) if t < 1.0 else float("inf")
    vals = []
    for s in range(0, steps + 1, 50):
        t = s * dt
        vals.append((t, grad(t)))
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D inviscid Burgers toy model, u0(x) = -sin(x)")
    lines.append("Characteristic gradient formula: min u_x(t) = -1/(1-t)")
    for t, g in vals:
        lines.append("  t = %.3f : min gradient = %.6f" % (t, g))
    lines.append("Blow-up time of the toy model: t = 1.0 (gradient diverges)")
    lines.append("This is a one-dimensional model only; the 3D Euler problem is different")
    return lines

print("Problem: Surface Quasi-Geostrophic Equation")
print("Statement: Do smooth solutions of the surface quasi-geostrophic equation remain smooth for all time, or can the temperature gradient blow up in finite time?")
print("Known: The critical dissipative equation is globally regular, and the subcritical case is also regular. For the inviscid equation, local existence is known and conditional regularity criteria exist, but global regularity is open. The equation is a leading model for the regularity problem.")
lines = compute()
print("\n".join(lines))
print("The public record carries no attempt. The Signature mirror record is JAH-USEQPDS-000014.")
