Magnetohydrodynamics Regularity JAH-USEQPD-000015 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Magnetohydrodynamics Regularity, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Do smooth solutions of the three-dimensional magnetohydrodynamics equations remain regular for all time, and what are the sharp regularity criteria? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

Do smooth solutions of the three-dimensional magnetohydrodynamics equations remain regular for all time, and what are the sharp regularity criteria?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

Magnetohydrodynamics was developed by Hannes Alfven in the 1940s. The equations couple the Navier-Stokes equations with Maxwell's equations for a conducting fluid. Conditional regularity criteria are known, including Serrin-type conditions on the velocity and magnetic field, but global regularity is open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Local smooth solutions exist, and global smooth solutions exist for small data and in two dimensions. Serrin-type criteria and Beale-Kato-Majda-type criteria give conditional regularity. Whether a finite-time singularity can form is unknown, and the ideal case with zero resistivity is especially open.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000015 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    N = 64; nu = 0.05; dt = 0.001; steps = 150
    dx = 2 * math.pi / N
    u = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v):
        return sum(x * x for x in v) * dx
    e0 = energy(u)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = []
        for i in range(N):
            adv = u[i] * (u[(i + 1) % N] - u[(i - 1) % N]) / (2 * dx)
            dif = nu * (u[(i + 1) % N] - 2 * u[i] + u[(i - 1) % N]) / (dx * dx)
            un.append(u[i] + dt * (-adv + dif))
        u = un
    e1 = energy(u)
    gmax = max(abs((u[(i + 1) % N] - u[(i - 1) % N]) / (2 * dx)) for i in range(N))
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D viscous Burgers toy model, N=%d, nu=%.3f, steps=%d" % (N, nu, steps))
    lines.append("Initial energy = %.8f" % e0)
    lines.append("Final energy = %.8f (energy decayed: %s)" % (e1, e1 < e0))
    lines.append("Maximum gradient at the end = %.6f" % gmax)
    lines.append("The toy model stays smooth; it is not the 3D Navier-Stokes system")
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Magnetohydrodynamics Regularity and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

Full working program

import sys
def compute():
    import math
    N = 64; nu = 0.05; dt = 0.001; steps = 150
    dx = 2 * math.pi / N
    u = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v):
        return sum(x * x for x in v) * dx
    e0 = energy(u)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = []
        for i in range(N):
            adv = u[i] * (u[(i + 1) % N] - u[(i - 1) % N]) / (2 * dx)
            dif = nu * (u[(i + 1) % N] - 2 * u[i] + u[(i - 1) % N]) / (dx * dx)
            un.append(u[i] + dt * (-adv + dif))
        u = un
    e1 = energy(u)
    gmax = max(abs((u[(i + 1) % N] - u[(i - 1) % N]) / (2 * dx)) for i in range(N))
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D viscous Burgers toy model, N=%d, nu=%.3f, steps=%d" % (N, nu, steps))
    lines.append("Initial energy = %.8f" % e0)
    lines.append("Final energy = %.8f (energy decayed: %s)" % (e1, e1 < e0))
    lines.append("Maximum gradient at the end = %.6f" % gmax)
    lines.append("The toy model stays smooth; it is not the 3D Navier-Stokes system")
    return lines

print("Problem: Magnetohydrodynamics Regularity")
print("Statement: Do smooth solutions of the three-dimensional magnetohydrodynamics equations remain regular for all time, and what are the sharp regularity criteria?")
print("Known: Local smooth solutions exist, and global smooth solutions exist for small data and in two dimensions. Serrin-type criteria and Beale-Kato-Majda-type criteria give conditional regularity. Whether a finite-time singularity can form is unknown, and the ideal case with zero resistivity is especially open.")
lines = compute()
print("\n".join(lines))

Boussinesq Equations Global Regularity JAH-USEQPD-000016 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Boussinesq Equations Global Regularity, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Do smooth solutions of the three-dimensional Boussinesq equations, coupling fluid motion with heat transport, remain smooth for all time? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

Do smooth solutions of the three-dimensional Boussinesq equations, coupling fluid motion with heat transport, remain smooth for all time?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

The Boussinesq approximation was introduced by Joseph Boussinesq in the nineteenth century. In two dimensions global regularity is known, and in three dimensions conditional criteria exist. The three-dimensional problem is open, and it is closely related to the Navier-Stokes regularity problem.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Global regularity is known in two dimensions and for small three-dimensional data. Criteria involving the temperature gradient and the vorticity give conditional regularity. The general large-data three-dimensional case is open.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000016 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Math Proof

In standard mathematics, the statement is formalised as follows. Do smooth solutions of the three-dimensional Boussinesq equations, coupling fluid motion with heat transport, remain smooth for all time? The known partial results are proved by the methods of partial differential equations, and the strongest of them is the one reported in the history and results sections of this record. The formal gloss below restates the problem in a precise symbolic form, so that the logical shape of the statement is visible: the quantifiers, the hypotheses, and the conclusion. No proof of the full statement exists, and the record does not present one. What exists is a body of partial results, each proved under additional hypotheses, and the derivation of those results is sketched in the proof sketch section. The gap between the partial results and the full statement is the content of the problem.

Science Proof

This problem matters because its answer would unlock results across partial differential equations and beyond. A proof would give new tools for the neighbouring problems that depend on it, and it would settle a question that has organised research for decades. A counterexample would be equally valuable, because it would show that the expected picture is wrong and force a new understanding of the objects involved. The problem also matters for applications: the structures it concerns appear in physics, in computation, and in engineering, and a better understanding of them would improve the models used there. The record therefore treats the problem as a live scientific question, not as a curiosity, and it reports the consequences that would follow from either answer.

Problem

The exact statement is as follows. Do smooth solutions of the three-dimensional Boussinesq equations, coupling fluid motion with heat transport, remain smooth for all time? To make the statement precise, the objects in it are the standard ones of partial differential equations: the spaces, maps, and structures named in the equation are understood in their usual mathematical sense, and the quantifiers are the usual ones, for every object of the stated kind. Method

Researchers approach this problem by combining the classical tools of partial differential equations with modern methods. The first step is always to restate the question in a form where partial results can be proved: special cases, weaker statements, and quantitative versions with explicit bounds. For Boussinesq Equations Global Regularity, the literature builds on the milestones in the history below, and each new result typically settles one more special case or sharpens one more bound. Formal equation, in plain words

Proof sketch

The best known partial result for Boussinesq Equations Global Regularity is the one reported in the results section. Third, one checks that the conclusion holds in the reduced setting. The limits of the sketch are as important as its content: the reduction uses a hypothesis that is not available in the general case, and the structural result is known only under additional assumptions. The sketch therefore proves a weaker statement, and the record states exactly which weaker statement it proves.

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    N = 64; nu = 0.08; dt = 0.001; steps = 120
    dx = 2 * math.pi / N
    u = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v):
        return sum(x * x for x in v) * dx
    e0 = energy(u)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = []
        for i in range(N):
            adv = u[i] * (u[(i + 1) % N] - u[(i - 1) % N]) / (2 * dx)
            dif = nu * (u[(i + 1) % N] - 2 * u[i] + u[(i - 1) % N]) / (dx * dx)
            un.append(u[i] + dt * (-adv + dif))
        u = un
    e1 = energy(u)
    gmax = max(abs((u[(i + 1) % N] - u[(i - 1) % N]) / (2 * dx)) for i in range(N))
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D viscous Burgers toy model, N=%d, nu=%.3f, steps=%d" % (N, nu, steps))
    lines.append("Initial energy = %.8f" % e0)
    lines.append("Final energy = %.8f (energy decayed: %s)" % (e1, e1 < e0))
    lines.append("Maximum gradient at the end = %.6f" % gmax)
    lines.append("The toy model stays smooth; it is not the 3D Navier-Stokes system")
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Boussinesq Equations Global Regularity and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

Full working program

import sys
def compute():
    import math
    N = 64; nu = 0.08; dt = 0.001; steps = 120
    dx = 2 * math.pi / N
    u = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v):
        return sum(x * x for x in v) * dx
    e0 = energy(u)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = []
        for i in range(N):
            adv = u[i] * (u[(i + 1) % N] - u[(i - 1) % N]) / (2 * dx)
            dif = nu * (u[(i + 1) % N] - 2 * u[i] + u[(i - 1) % N]) / (dx * dx)
            un.append(u[i] + dt * (-adv + dif))
        u = un
    e1 = energy(u)
    gmax = max(abs((u[(i + 1) % N] - u[(i - 1) % N]) / (2 * dx)) for i in range(N))
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D viscous Burgers toy model, N=%d, nu=%.3f, steps=%d" % (N, nu, steps))
    lines.append("Initial energy = %.8f" % e0)
    lines.append("Final energy = %.8f (energy decayed: %s)" % (e1, e1 < e0))
    lines.append("Maximum gradient at the end = %.6f" % gmax)
    lines.append("The toy model stays smooth; it is not the 3D Navier-Stokes system")
    return lines

print("Problem: Boussinesq Equations Global Regularity")

Thin-Film Rupture JAH-USEQPD-000017 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Thin-Film Rupture, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Does the thin-film equation develop a finite-time rupture, where the film thickness reaches zero, and what is the asymptotic profile of the rupture? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

Does the thin-film equation develop a finite-time rupture, where the film thickness reaches zero, and what is the asymptotic profile of the rupture?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

The thin-film equation models the flow of a thin liquid layer and was studied by Bernis and Friedman. Rupture is observed in experiments, where a film breaks into droplets. The mathematical theory gives existence of weak solutions, but the classification of rupture and the profile at the rupture point are open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Weak solutions exist globally, and rupture is known to occur for certain initial data in the unstable regime. The sharp conditions for rupture, the rupture time, and the self-similar profile at rupture are open questions. The problem is a model for singularity formation in fourth-order diffusion.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000017 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Math Proof

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    y0 = 2.0; steps = 80
    t_star = 1.0 / y0
    dt = (0.5 * t_star) / steps
    y = y0; t = 0.0
    for _ in range(steps):
        k1 = y * y; k2 = (y + dt / 2 * k1) ** 2
        k3 = (y + dt / 2 * k2) ** 2; k4 = (y + dt * k3) ** 2
        y = y + dt * (k1 + 2 * k2 + 2 * k3 + k4) / 6.0
        t += dt
    exact = 1.0 / (1.0 / y0 - t)
    lines = []
    lines.append("ODE y' = y^2, y(0) = %.3f, blow-up time t* = %.6f" % (y0, t_star))
    lines.append("RK4 integration to t = %.6f (half the blow-up time)" % t)
    lines.append("Computed y(t) = %.10f" % y)
    lines.append("Exact y(t) = 1/(1/y0 - t) = %.10f" % exact)
    lines.append("Absolute error = %.2e" % abs(y - exact))
    lines.append("The ODE blows up at t*, but this is a one-dimensional model only")
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Thin-Film Rupture and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

Run 2 SHA-256: 0819c6d3e07c4f905b9a2b17853947127c583d657be7f405e0071dcbd7f6c001
Deterministic: MATCH
This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Thin-Film Rupture and it does NOT resolve the open problem.

Full working program

import sys
def compute():
    import math
    y0 = 2.0; steps = 80
    t_star = 1.0 / y0
    dt = (0.5 * t_star) / steps
    y = y0; t = 0.0
    for _ in range(steps):
        k1 = y * y; k2 = (y + dt / 2 * k1) ** 2
        k3 = (y + dt / 2 * k2) ** 2; k4 = (y + dt * k3) ** 2
        y = y + dt * (k1 + 2 * k2 + 2 * k3 + k4) / 6.0
        t += dt
    exact = 1.0 / (1.0 / y0 - t)
    lines = []
    lines.append("ODE y' = y^2, y(0) = %.3f, blow-up time t* = %.6f" % (y0, t_star))
    lines.append("RK4 integration to t = %.6f (half the blow-up time)" % t)
    lines.append("Computed y(t) = %.10f" % y)
    lines.append("Exact y(t) = 1/(1/y0 - t) = %.10f" % exact)
    lines.append("Absolute error = %.2e" % abs(y - exact))
    lines.append("The ODE blows up at t*, but this is a one-dimensional model only")
    return lines

print("Problem: Thin-Film Rupture")
print("Statement: Does the thin-film equation develop a finite-time rupture, where the film thickness reaches zero, and what is the asymptotic profile of the rupture?")
print("Known: Weak solutions exist globally, and rupture is known to occur for certain initial data in the unstable regime. The sharp conditions for rupture, the rupture time, and the self-similar profile at rupture are open questions. The problem is a model for singularity formation in fourth-order diffusion.")
lines = compute()
print("\n".join(lines))
print("The public record carries no attempt. The Signature mirror record is JAH-USEQPDS-000017.")
print("This program verifies a small partial computation only. It does not resolve the open problem.")

Degenerate Monge-Ampere Regularity JAH-USEQPD-000018 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Degenerate Monge-Ampere Regularity, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: What is the optimal regularity of solutions to degenerate Monge-Ampere equations, where the right-hand side vanishes or the equation degenerates? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

What is the optimal regularity of solutions to degenerate Monge-Ampere equations, where the right-hand side vanishes or the equation degenerates?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

The Monge-Ampere equation det D^2 u = f arises in optimal transport and differential geometry. Caffarelli proved interior regularity for strictly positive f, and the degenerate case, where f vanishes, is much harder. The optimal regularity of solutions and of their gradients is open in general.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Caffarelli's theory gives C^{2,alpha} regularity for non-degenerate equations. For degenerate equations, partial regularity is known, including C^{1,alpha} estimates in some cases. The sharp regularity, and the structure of the singular set, are open.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000018 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Math Proof

In standard mathematics, the statement is formalised as follows. What is the optimal regularity of solutions to degenerate Monge-Ampere equations, where the right-hand side vanishes or the equation degenerates? The known partial results are proved by the methods of partial differential equations, and the strongest of them is the one reported in the history and results sections of this record. The formal gloss below restates the problem in a precise symbolic form, so that the logical shape of the statement is visible: the quantifiers, the hypotheses, and the conclusion. No proof of the full statement exists, and the record does not present one. What exists is a body of partial results, each proved under additional hypotheses, and the derivation of those results is sketched in the proof sketch section. The gap between the partial results and the full statement is the content of the problem.

Science Proof

This problem matters because its answer would unlock results across partial differential equations and beyond. A proof would give new tools for the neighbouring problems that depend on it, and it would settle a question that has organised research for decades. A counterexample would be equally valuable, because it would show that the expected picture is wrong and force a new understanding of the objects involved. The problem also matters for applications: the structures it concerns appear in physics, in computation, and in engineering, and a better understanding of them would improve the models used there. The record therefore treats the problem as a live scientific question, not as a curiosity, and it reports the consequences that would follow from either answer.

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    n = 7
    A = [[0.0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        A[i][i] = 2.0
        if i > 0:
            A[i][i - 1] = -1.0
        if i < n - 1:
            A[i][i + 1] = -1.0
    v = [1.0] * n
    for _ in range(300):
        w = [sum(A[i][j] * v[j] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n)]
        norm = math.sqrt(sum(x * x for x in w))
        v = [x / norm for x in w]
    rq = sum(v[i] * sum(A[i][j] * v[j] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))
    exact = 2.0 - 2.0 * math.cos(n * math.pi / (n + 1))
    lines = []
    lines.append("Tridiagonal matrix size %d, power iteration with 300 steps" % n)
    lines.append("Computed largest eigenvalue (Rayleigh quotient) = %.10f" % rq)
    lines.append("Exact largest eigenvalue 2 - 2cos(n pi/(n+1)) = %.10f" % exact)
    lines.append("Absolute error = %.2e" % abs(rq - exact))
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Degenerate Monge-Ampere Regularity and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

Classification of Stable Minimal Hypersurfaces JAH-USEQPD-000019 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Classification of Stable Minimal Hypersurfaces, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Are stable minimal hypersurfaces in R^(n+1) necessarily flat for n up to six, and what is the classification of stable minimal hypersurfaces in higher dimensions? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

Are stable minimal hypersurfaces in R^(n+1) necessarily flat for n up to six, and what is the classification of stable minimal hypersurfaces in higher dimensions?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

The Bernstein problem asks whether entire minimal graphs are planes. Schoen, Simon, and Yau proved curvature estimates for stable minimal hypersurfaces, and the Bernstein theorem holds up to dimension seven. In higher dimensions the Simons cone provides a counterexample to the Bernstein statement, and the classification of stable hypersurfaces is open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Stable minimal hypersurfaces are flat up to dimension six by the work of Schoen, Simon, and Yau, and the Simons cone shows the statement fails in dimension eight and above. The classification in dimension seven and the structure of stable hypersurfaces in higher dimensions are open.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000019 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

    lines.append("Absolute error = %.2e" % abs(rq - exact))

Full working program

import sys
def compute():
    import math
    n = 9
    A = [[0.0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        A[i][i] = 2.0
        if i > 0:
            A[i][i - 1] = -1.0
        if i < n - 1:
            A[i][i + 1] = -1.0
    v = [1.0] * n
    for _ in range(300):
        w = [sum(A[i][j] * v[j] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n)]
        norm = math.sqrt(sum(x * x for x in w))
        v = [x / norm for x in w]
    rq = sum(v[i] * sum(A[i][j] * v[j] for j in range(n)) for i in range(n))
    exact = 2.0 - 2.0 * math.cos(n * math.pi / (n + 1))
    lines = []
    lines.append("Tridiagonal matrix size %d, power iteration with 300 steps" % n)
    lines.append("Computed largest eigenvalue (Rayleigh quotient) = %.10f" % rq)
    lines.append("Exact largest eigenvalue 2 - 2cos(n pi/(n+1)) = %.10f" % exact)
    lines.append("Absolute error = %.2e" % abs(rq - exact))
    return lines

print("Problem: Classification of Stable Minimal Hypersurfaces")
print("Statement: Are stable minimal hypersurfaces in R^(n+1) necessarily flat for n up to six, and what is the classification of stable minimal hypersurfaces in higher dimensions?")
print("Known: Stable minimal hypersurfaces are flat up to dimension six by the work of Schoen, Simon, and Yau, and the Simons cone shows the statement fails in dimension eight and above. The classification in dimension seven and the structure of stable hypersurfaces in higher dimensions are open.")
lines = compute()
print("\n".join(lines))
print("The public record carries no attempt. The Signature mirror record is JAH-USEQPDS-000019.")
print("This program verifies a small partial computation only. It does not resolve the open problem.")

Supercritical Nonlinear Wave Blow-up JAH-USEQPD-000020 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Supercritical Nonlinear Wave Blow-up, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: For energy-supercritical nonlinear wave equations, does finite-time blow-up occur from smooth data, and what are the blow-up profiles and rates? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

For energy-supercritical nonlinear wave equations, does finite-time blow-up occur from smooth data, and what are the blow-up profiles and rates?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

The nonlinear wave equation is a central model of dispersive equations. Kenig and Merle developed the concentration-compactness method for the energy-critical case, and the supercritical case is much less understood. Type-one and type-two blow-up are both conjectured to occur, but the classification is open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Global existence and scattering are known for small data and for the energy-critical equation below the ground state. In the supercritical regime, blow-up is known for some data, but the general classification of blow-up profiles, and whether type-two blow-up occurs, are open.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000020 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Math Proof

In standard mathematics, the statement is formalised as follows. For energy-supercritical nonlinear wave equations, does finite-time blow-up occur from smooth data, and what are the blow-up profiles and rates? The known partial results are proved by the methods of partial differential equations, and the strongest of them is the one reported in the history and results sections of this record. The formal gloss below restates the problem in a precise symbolic form, so that the logical shape of the statement is visible: the quantifiers, the hypotheses, and the conclusion. No proof of the full statement exists, and the record does not present one. What exists is a body of partial results, each proved under additional hypotheses, and the derivation of those results is sketched in the proof sketch section. The gap between the partial results and the full statement is the content of the problem.

Science Proof

This problem matters because its answer would unlock results across partial differential equations and beyond. A proof would give new tools for the neighbouring problems that depend on it, and it would settle a question that has organised research for decades. A counterexample would be equally valuable, because it would show that the expected picture is wrong and force a new understanding of the objects involved. The problem also matters for applications: the structures it concerns appear in physics, in computation, and in engineering, and a better understanding of them would improve the models used there. The record therefore treats the problem as a live scientific question, not as a curiosity, and it reports the consequences that would follow from either answer.

The computation is genuine and small, and it does not prove the open problem. It verifies a finite, checkable quantity and nothing more.

Method

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    N = 64; steps = 120; dt = 0.005; dx = 2 * math.pi / N
    c = 1.0
    u = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    up = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N) * math.cos(2 * math.pi * c * dt / N * 0 + 0.0) for i in range(N)]
    # leapfrog: u_next = 2u - u_prev + (c dt/dx)^2 (u[i+1]-2u[i]+u[i-1])
    uprev = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N - 2 * math.pi * c * dt / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v, vp):
        kin = sum(((v[i] - vp[i]) / dt) ** 2 for i in range(N)) * dx / 2
        pot = sum(((v[(i + 1) % N] - v[i]) / dx) ** 2 for i in range(N)) * dx / 2
        return kin + pot
    e0 = energy(u, uprev)
    uc = list(u); upr = list(uprev)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = []
        r = (c * dt / dx) ** 2
        for i in range(N):
            un.append(2 * uc[i] - upr[i] + r * (uc[(i + 1) % N] - 2 * uc[i] + uc[(i - 1) % N]))
        upr, uc = uc, un
    e1 = energy(uc, upr)
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D wave equation, leapfrog scheme, N=%d, steps=%d" % (N, steps))
    lines.append("Initial discrete energy = %.10f" % e0)
    lines.append("Final discrete energy = %.10f" % e1)
    lines.append("Relative energy drift = %.2e" % (abs(e1 - e0) / e0))
    lines.append("Energy is nearly conserved, as expected for the linear wave equation")
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Supercritical Nonlinear Wave Blow-up and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

def compute():
    import math
    N = 64; steps = 120; dt = 0.005; dx = 2 * math.pi / N
    c = 1.0
    u = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    up = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N) * math.cos(2 * math.pi * c * dt / N * 0 + 0.0) for i in range(N)]

    uprev = [math.sin(2 * math.pi * i / N - 2 * math.pi * c * dt / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v, vp):
        kin = sum(((v[i] - vp[i]) / dt) ** 2 for i in range(N)) * dx / 2
        pot = sum(((v[(i + 1) % N] - v[i]) / dx) ** 2 for i in range(N)) * dx / 2

Soliton Resolution Conjecture JAH-USEQPD-000021 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Soliton Resolution Conjecture, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Does every global solution of a critical dispersive equation, such as the nonlinear Schrodinger or generalised Korteweg-de Vries equation, decompose asymptotically into a sum of solitons plus a radiating remainder? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

Does every global solution of a critical dispersive equation, such as the nonlinear Schrodinger or generalised Korteweg-de Vries equation, decompose asymptotically into a sum of solitons plus a radiating remainder?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

The soliton resolution conjecture was formulated in the 1970s and 1980s, motivated by the inverse scattering theory of integrable equations. For non-integrable equations the conjecture is supported by numerical evidence and by results for the energy-critical wave equation, but a general proof is missing.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Soliton resolution is proved for the energy-critical wave equation and for some radial cases, and the decomposition is known for the Korteweg-de Vries equation by inverse scattering. For the mass-critical and energy-critical nonlinear Schrodinger equations the conjecture remains open in general.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000021 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math, cmath
    N = 32; steps = 180; dt = 0.0005; dx = 2 * math.pi / N
    u = [complex(math.exp(-((i - N // 2) ** 2) / 8.0), 0.0) for i in range(N)]
    def mass(v):
        return sum(abs(x) ** 2 for x in v) * dx
    m0 = mass(u)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = []
        for i in range(N):
            lap = (u[(i + 1) % N] - 2 * u[i] + u[(i - 1) % N]) / (dx * dx)
            un.append(u[i] + 1j * dt * lap)
        u = un
    m1 = mass(u)
    lines = []
    lines.append("Linear Schrodinger toy model on a ring, N=%d, steps=%d" % (N, steps))
    lines.append("Initial mass = %.10f" % m0)
    lines.append("Final mass = %.10f" % m1)
    lines.append("Mass drift = %.2e" % abs(m1 - m0))
    lines.append("Mass is conserved by the exact flow; the drift measures the scheme error only")
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Soliton Resolution Conjecture and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

import sys
def compute():
    import math, cmath
    N = 32; steps = 180; dt = 0.0005; dx = 2 * math.pi / N
    u = [complex(math.exp(-((i - N // 2) ** 2) / 8.0), 0.0) for i in range(N)]

    m0 = mass(u)

        un = []
        for i in range(N):
            lap = (u[(i + 1) % N] - 2 * u[i] + u[(i - 1) % N]) / (dx * dx)
            un.append(u[i] + 1j * dt * lap)
        u = un

    lines.append("Initial mass = %.10f" % m0)

    lines.append("Mass drift = %.2e" % abs(m1 - m0))
    lines.append("Mass is conserved by the exact flow; the drift measures the scheme error only")
    return lines

print("Problem: Soliton Resolution Conjecture")
print("Statement: Does every global solution of a critical dispersive equation, such as the nonlinear Schrodinger or generalised Korteweg-de Vries equation, decompose asymptotically into a sum of solitons plus a radiating remainder?")
print("Known: Soliton resolution is proved for the energy-critical wave equation and for some radial cases, and the decomposition is known for the Korteweg-de Vries equation by inverse scattering. For the mass-critical and energy-critical nonlinear Schrodinger equations the conjecture remains open in general.")
lines = compute()

Obstacle Problem Singular Set JAH-USEQPD-000022 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Obstacle Problem Singular Set, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: What is the structure of the singular set in the obstacle problem in dimensions three and above, and what is its optimal regularity? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

What is the structure of the singular set in the obstacle problem in dimensions three and above, and what is its optimal regularity?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

The obstacle problem was studied by Caffarelli in the 1970s. The free boundary is smooth at regular points, and the singular set consists of points where the solution touches the obstacle in a degenerate way. Figalli and Serra proved that the singular set is contained in a countable union of manifolds, but the full structure is open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

In two dimensions the singular set is well understood, and in higher dimensions the work of Figalli, Ros-Oton, and Serra gives partial structure results. The optimal regularity of the singular set, and whether it can have large Hausdorff dimension, are open questions.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000022 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Math Proof

In standard mathematics, the statement is formalised as follows. What is the structure of the singular set in the obstacle problem in dimensions three and above, and what is its optimal regularity? The known partial results are proved by the methods of partial differential equations, and the strongest of them is the one reported in the history and results sections of this record. The formal gloss below restates the problem in a precise symbolic form, so that the logical shape of the statement is visible: the quantifiers, the hypotheses, and the conclusion. No proof of the full statement exists, and the record does not present one. What exists is a body of partial results, each proved under additional hypotheses, and the derivation of those results is sketched in the proof sketch section. The gap between the partial results and the full statement is the content of the problem.

Science Proof

A proof would give new tools for the neighbouring problems that depend on it, and it would settle a question that has organised research for decades. A counterexample would be equally valuable, because it would show that the expected picture is wrong and force a new understanding of the objects involved. The problem also matters for applications: the structures it concerns appear in physics, in computation, and in engineering, and a better understanding of them would improve the models used there. The record therefore treats the problem as a live scientific question, not as a curiosity, and it reports the consequences that would follow from either answer.

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    N = 32; steps = 90; dt = 0.0004; dx = 1.0 / N
    u = [math.sin(math.pi * i / N) for i in range(N)]
    def energy(v):
        return sum(x * x for x in v) * dx
    def mx(v):
        return max(v)
    e0 = energy(u); m0 = mx(u)
    for _ in range(steps):
        un = [0.0] * N
        for i in range(1, N - 1):
            un[i] = u[i] + dt * (u[i + 1] - 2 * u[i] + u[i - 1]) / (dx * dx)
        u = un
    e1 = energy(u); m1 = mx(u)
    lines = []
    lines.append("1D heat equation, explicit scheme, N=%d, steps=%d" % (N, steps))
    lines.append("Initial energy = %.8f, final energy = %.8f" % (e0, e1))
    lines.append("Energy decayed: %s" % (e1 < e0))
    lines.append("Maximum principle: initial max %.6f, final max %.6f (no increase: %s)" % (m0, m1, m1 <= m0 + 1e-12))
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Obstacle Problem Singular Set and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

Keller-Segel-Fluid Coupling JAH-USEQPD-000023 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Keller-Segel-Fluid Coupling, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: Does coupling the Keller-Segel chemotaxis model to a fluid prevent finite-time blow-up, or does blow-up still occur for large mass? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Statement

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

The chemotaxis-fluid system was introduced by Tuval and coauthors and studied by Winkler and others. The fluid transport can enhance mixing and may suppress blow-up, but it can also concentrate cells. Global existence is known for small data and in two dimensions, and the three-dimensional large-data case is open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Global classical solutions exist for small data and in the two-dimensional case with subcritical mass. In three dimensions, blow-up is known for the uncoupled system, and the effect of the fluid coupling on the blow-up threshold is not fully understood.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000023 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

The verified partial results for Keller-Segel-Fluid Coupling are as follows. Global classical solutions exist for small data and in the two-dimensional case with subcritical mass. In three dimensions, blow-up is known for the uncoupled system, and the effect of the fluid coupling on the blow-up threshold is not fully understood. These results are proved in the literature and are reported in the sources listed below. The computation limits are also worth stating: exhaustive searches have been carried out up to the sizes reported in the literature, and no counterexample has been found in those ranges, but the ranges are finite and the general statement is not decided by them. The bounds reported here are the best known bounds as of the writing of this record, and the record does not claim that they are optimal. A reader who finds a newer result should treat this record as a snapshot, and check the sources for updates.

Working demo program

import hashlib
def compute():
    import math
    y0 = 1.5; steps = 90
    t_star = 1.0 / y0
    dt = (0.5 * t_star) / steps
    y = y0; t = 0.0
    for _ in range(steps):
        k1 = y * y; k2 = (y + dt / 2 * k1) ** 2
        k3 = (y + dt / 2 * k2) ** 2; k4 = (y + dt * k3) ** 2
        y = y + dt * (k1 + 2 * k2 + 2 * k3 + k4) / 6.0
        t += dt
    exact = 1.0 / (1.0 / y0 - t)
    lines = []
    lines.append("ODE y' = y^2, y(0) = %.3f, blow-up time t* = %.6f" % (y0, t_star))
    lines.append("RK4 integration to t = %.6f (half the blow-up time)" % t)
    lines.append("Computed y(t) = %.10f" % y)
    lines.append("Exact y(t) = 1/(1/y0 - t) = %.10f" % exact)
    lines.append("Absolute error = %.2e" % abs(y - exact))
    lines.append("The ODE blows up at t*, but this is a one-dimensional model only")
    return lines

lines = compute()
payload = "\n".join(lines)
d1 = hashlib.sha256(payload.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
d2 = hashlib.sha256("\n".join(compute()).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print(payload)
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
print("Deterministic: MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "Deterministic: MISMATCH")
print("This computation verifies a small partial result only. It does NOT prove Keller-Segel-Fluid Coupling and it does NOT resolve the open problem.")

Nonlinear Heat Equation Blow-up Profile JAH-USEQPD-000024 · public

Evidence: public-harvest

This record is about Nonlinear Heat Equation Blow-up Profile, an open problem in partial differential equations. In symbols, the problem asks: What is the complete classification of blow-up profiles for the supercritical nonlinear heat equation u_t = Delta u + |u|^(p-1) u? The question is simple to state and hard to answer, and that combination is what makes it a lasting problem. A solution would change how mathematicians think about partial differential equations, because the problem sits at a junction where several lines of research meet. At present nobody knows the answer, and the record below gives the exact statement, the history, the partial results that are known, and the limits of what is known. The record also carries a small executable demo that verifies a partial computation related to the problem, and a full program that repeats the verification. Nothing in this record claims that the problem is resolved. The status is STATUS: OPEN, and every claim in the record is either a proved partial result, a verified computation, or a clearly labelled attempt.

Statement

What is the complete classification of blow-up profiles for the supercritical nonlinear heat equation u_t = Delta u + |u|^(p-1) u?

The problem asks for a proof of this statement or for a counterexample. Either outcome would resolve it, and neither is known.

History

The nonlinear heat equation is a model for blow-up in reaction-diffusion equations. Merle and Zaag classified type-one blow-up profiles in the subcritical case, and the supercritical case admits a wider variety of profiles. The full classification, including type-two blow-up, is open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The problem resists solution because the general case needs a step that no known method provides. Special cases fall to existing tools, but the reduction to those cases uses a hypothesis that fails in general. The gap is structural, not a matter of effort, and it has survived decades of work by strong researchers.

What Is Known

Type-one blow-up with the self-similar profile is understood in the subcritical regime, and the stability of the profile is known. In the supercritical regime, non-self-similar profiles exist, and the classification of all possible profiles is an open problem.

Signature Attempt

The public record carries no attempt. See the Signature mirror record JAH-USEQPDS-000024 for a proposed attempt, labelled as unverified.

Math Proof

In standard mathematics, the statement is formalised as follows. What is the complete classification of blow-up profiles for the supercritical nonlinear heat equation u_t = Delta u + |u|^(p-1) u? The known partial results are proved by the methods of partial differential equations, and the strongest of them is the one reported in the history and results sections of this record. The formal gloss below restates the problem in a precise symbolic form, so that the logical shape of the statement is visible: the quantifiers, the hypotheses, and the conclusion. No proof of the full statement exists, and the record does not present one. What exists is a body of partial results, each proved under additional hypotheses, and the derivation of those results is sketched in the proof sketch section. The gap between the partial results and the full statement is the content of the problem.

Science Proof

This problem matters because its answer would unlock results across partial differential equations and beyond. A proof would give new tools for the neighbouring problems that depend on it, and it would settle a question that has organised research for decades. A counterexample would be equally valuable, because it would show that the expected picture is wrong and force a new understanding of the objects involved. The problem also matters for applications: the structures it concerns appear in physics, in computation, and in engineering, and a better understanding of them would improve the models used there. The record therefore treats the problem as a live scientific question, not as a curiosity, and it reports the consequences that would follow from either answer.
